<a id='question-132'></a>

### Question 132: Granulomatous Hepatitis
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Systemic dissemination of attenuated Mycobacterium bovis (BCG) following intravesical immunotherapy for bladder cancer can cause granulomatous hepatitis, characterized histologically by non-caseating granulomas and clinically by fever, fatigue, and elevated liver enzymes. (膀胱内BCG免疫治疗可能导致结核分枝杆菌的全身播散，引起粒状性肝炎，组织学上表现为非干酪样肉芽肿，临床上表现为发热、疲劳和肝酶升高。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 70-year-old male with a history of high-grade, non-muscle invasive urothelial carcinoma of the bladder is undergoing standard treatment with intravesical Bacillus Calmette-Guérin (BCG) instillations. He has completed three weekly instillations of a six-week course. Three weeks after initiating BCG therapy, he presents to his primary care physician complaining of persistent low-grade fevers (up to 38.5°C), profound fatigue, and anorexia. Laboratory tests reveal elevated liver transaminases (AST 350 U/L, ALT 400 U/L) and mild leukocytosis (WBC 12,000/µL) with a neutrophilic predominance. His bilirubin is normal. Blood cultures for common bacteria and urine cultures are negative. A liver biopsy is performed, which shows multiple non-caseating epithelioid granulomas scattered throughout the hepatic parenchyma, without evidence of significant necrosis or acid-fast bacilli staining. Which of the following is the most likely underlying mechanism causing this patient's liver pathology?

(A) Immune-mediated injury triggered by hepatocellular autoantigens, leading to chronic active hepatitis.
(B) Direct viral cytopathic effect of a hepatotropic virus, causing hepatocellular necrosis and inflammation.
(C) Systemic dissemination of attenuated Mycobacterium bovis (BCG) following intravesical instillation, inducing a granulomatous inflammatory response.
(D) Drug-induced liver injury (DILI) secondary to a medication used for his bladder cancer, causing cholestatic hepatitis.
(E) Metastatic deposition of urothelial carcinoma cells to the liver, resulting in space-occupying lesions and biliary obstruction.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Pathophysiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 70-year-old male with high-grade non-muscle invasive urothelial carcinoma. This establishes the context for potential treatments.
*   **Treatment:** Intravesical BCG instillations. This is a crucial piece of information, as BCG is a live attenuated strain of *Mycobacterium bovis*.
*   **Timeline:** Symptoms (fever, fatigue, anorexia) and elevated transaminases developed three weeks *after* initiating BCG therapy. This temporal relationship strongly suggests a link to the treatment.
*   **Symptoms:** Persistent low-grade fevers, fatigue, anorexia are systemic symptoms often associated with infections or inflammatory conditions.
*   **Lab Findings:** Elevated AST/ALT (hepatocellular injury pattern), mild leukocytosis (inflammation/infection), normal bilirubin (no significant cholestasis or severe hepatocellular failure). Negative routine blood and urine cultures rule out common bacterial infections.
*   **Liver Biopsy:** Multiple non-caseating epithelioid granulomas. This is the key histological finding. Granulomas are characteristic of certain infections (like tuberculosis, fungal infections, sarcoidosis) and some inflammatory conditions. The *non-caseating* nature is important.
*   **Negative AFB Stain:** Absence of acid-fast bacilli on biopsy doesn't rule out mycobacterial infection, especially with attenuated strains or low burden.

These clues converge on the diagnosis of BCG-induced granulomatous hepatitis, a known complication of intravesical BCG therapy. The attenuated *Mycobacterium bovis* can disseminate systemically, particularly in immunocompromised or elderly patients, leading to granuloma formation in various organs, including the liver.

##### 2. Pathophysiological Deep-Dive (病理生理机制深度剖析)
1.  **Initiating Stimulus:** Intravesical instillation of live attenuated *Mycobacterium bovis* (BCG) for bladder cancer immunotherapy.
2.  **Mechanism of Dissemination:** BCG is designed to stimulate an immune response within the bladder wall. However, in some cases, the attenuated mycobacteria can cross the urothelial barrier and enter the bloodstream (systemic dissemination). This is more common in older patients or those with underlying conditions.
3.  **Immune Response:** Once in the systemic circulation, BCG organisms are phagocytosed by macrophages. The macrophages attempt to contain the infection but are unable to fully eradicate the mycobacteria due to their intracellular location and resistance to typical macrophage killing mechanisms.
4.  **Granuloma Formation:** The persistent presence of BCG within macrophages, coupled with the release of cytokines (e.g., IFN-γ, TNF-α) by activated T cells (primarily CD4+ Th1 cells), leads to the recruitment of more macrophages and lymphocytes. These cells aggregate around the central core of BCG-infected macrophages, forming characteristic epithelioid granulomas.
5.  **Histopathology:** The liver biopsy shows multiple non-caseating epithelioid granulomas. "Non-caseating" means there is no central area of necrosis (cheese-like appearance), which is typical of *Mycobacterium tuberculosis* infection in immunocompetent hosts. BCG-induced granulomas are often non-caseating or show minimal central necrosis. Acid-fast bacilli (AFB) staining may be negative or difficult to find.
6.  **Clinical Manifestations:** The systemic inflammatory response triggered by the disseminated BCG and the resulting granulomatous inflammation in the liver cause symptoms like fever, fatigue, and elevated liver enzymes (AST/ALT), reflecting hepatocellular injury.
7.  **Treatment:** BCG-induced systemic infection, including hepatitis, typically responds well to standard antituberculous therapy (isoniazid, rifampin, ethambutol), although *M. bovis* is inherently resistant to pyrazinamide.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Immune-mediated injury triggered by hepatocellular autoantigens, leading to chronic active hepatitis.** This describes autoimmune hepatitis. While autoimmune hepatitis can cause elevated transaminases and non-caseating granulomas (though less common than in infections), it is not directly linked to the recent BCG instillation. Autoimmune hepatitis typically presents more insidiously and is associated with specific autoantibodies (ANA, anti-smooth muscle antibody). The temporal link to BCG makes this less likely.
- (B): **Direct viral cytopathic effect of a hepatotropic virus, causing hepatocellular necrosis and inflammation.** This describes acute viral hepatitis (e.g., Hepatitis A, B, C, E). Viral hepatitis typically causes more pronounced hepatocellular necrosis, often with ballooning degeneration, and may show specific viral inclusions or immunohistochemical staining. While viral hepatitis can cause fever and elevated transaminases, the granulomatous pattern on biopsy is not characteristic, and there's no link to the BCG therapy.
- (D): **Drug-induced liver injury (DILI) secondary to a medication used for his bladder cancer, causing cholestatic hepatitis.** DILI is a possibility with any medication. However, the histological finding of non-caseating granulomas is uncommon in typical DILI patterns (hepatocellular, cholestatic, or mixed). While some drugs can cause granulomatous hepatitis, the strong temporal association with BCG instillation points away from DILI as the primary cause. Cholestatic hepatitis would typically present with elevated alkaline phosphatase and bilirubin, which are normal in this patient.
- (E): **Metastatic deposition of urothelial carcinoma cells to the liver, resulting in space-occupying lesions and biliary obstruction.** Liver metastases from urothelial carcinoma are possible, but they typically present as discrete nodules or masses on imaging and histology, not diffuse granulomas. Metastases usually do not cause systemic symptoms like fever and fatigue in this manner, nor do they typically cause a diffuse hepatocellular injury pattern with elevated transaminases unless biliary obstruction occurs, which is not indicated here (normal bilirubin).

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
BCG immunotherapy for bladder cancer can lead to systemic dissemination of the attenuated *Mycobacterium bovis*, causing granulomatous inflammation in various organs, including the liver (

<a id='question-133'></a>

### Question 133: Chronic Granulomatous Gastritis
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The question tests the recognition of isolated non-caseating granulomas in the gastric mucosa, their differential diagnosis (sarcoidosis, Crohn's disease, idiopathic), and the appropriate diagnostic approach when systemic disease is suspected. (考察胃黏膜孤立的非干酪样肉芽肿的识别、鉴别诊断（结节病、克罗恩病、特发性）以及怀疑全身性疾病时的诊断方法。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 46-year-old woman presents to a gastroenterology clinic complaining of persistent dyspepsia for the past 6 months, characterized by intermittent epigastric discomfort and bloating, unrelated to meals. She denies nausea, vomiting, weight loss, or hematemesis. Her past medical history is unremarkable, and she takes no medications. Physical examination is normal. Upper endoscopy reveals multiple irregular, pale, nodular mucosal elevations primarily in the gastric antrum, without evidence of ulceration or bleeding. Biopsies taken from these lesions show intact gastric mucosa with prominent, non-caseating epithelioid granulomas within the lamina propria. Acid-fast bacilli (AFB) stains and Gomori methenamine silver (GMS) stains for fungi are negative. Histology is negative for *Helicobacter pylori*. A subsequent comprehensive systemic evaluation, including chest imaging and serum angiotensin-converting enzyme (ACE) levels, reveals bilateral hilar lymphadenopathy and elevated ACE levels, consistent with subclinical pulmonary sarcoidosis. Which of the following is the most likely underlying cause of the gastric granulomas in this patient?

(A) *Helicobacter pylori* infection leading to chronic active gastritis with granuloma formation.
(B) Crohn disease manifesting with skip lesions involving the stomach.
(C) Tuberculosis causing gastric involvement.
(D) Sarcoidosis with gastric manifestation.
(E) Idiopathic granulomatous gastritis unrelated to systemic disease.

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Pathophysiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 46-year-old female with persistent dyspepsia. This age group is common for both sarcoidosis and Crohn's disease, although *H. pylori* is also prevalent.
- **Symptoms:** Dyspepsia (epigastric discomfort, bloating) is non-specific but common in gastric pathology. Absence of alarm symptoms (weight loss, vomiting, bleeding) makes malignancy less likely but doesn't rule out inflammatory conditions.
- **Endoscopic Findings:** Multiple irregular, pale, nodular mucosal elevations in the antrum. This morphology is suggestive of granulomatous inflammation, but can also be seen in other conditions. Absence of ulceration is noted.
- **Biopsy Findings:** Intact mucosa with non-caseating epithelioid granulomas in the lamina propria. This is the crucial finding. Non-caseating granulomas are characteristic of sarcoidosis, Crohn's disease, and some infections (like TB, though typically caseating) or idiopathic conditions.
- **Negative Stains:** AFB and GMS stains are negative, ruling out mycobacterial (TB) and fungal infections as the primary cause of the granulomas.
- **Negative *H. pylori*:** Rules out *H. pylori* gastritis as the direct cause, although *H. pylori* can sometimes be associated with granulomas.
- **Systemic Workup:** Bilateral hilar lymphadenopathy and elevated ACE levels confirm subclinical pulmonary sarcoidosis. This strongly links the gastric findings to a systemic granulomatous disease.

##### 2. Pathophysiological Deep-Dive (病理生理机制深度剖析)
- **Sarcoidosis:** A systemic disease characterized by the formation of non-caseating epithelioid granulomas in various organs. The exact etiology is unknown, but it involves an exaggerated T-helper cell (Th1) response to an unidentified antigen in genetically susceptible individuals.
- **Gastric Involvement in Sarcoidosis:** While the lungs and lymph nodes are most commonly affected, sarcoidosis can involve the gastrointestinal tract, including the stomach, in about 1-5% of cases. Gastric sarcoidosis typically presents as non-caseating granulomas in the lamina propria, often in the antrum, without significant ulceration or fibrosis. The presence of gastric granulomas in a patient with confirmed systemic sarcoidosis (as evidenced by pulmonary findings and elevated ACE) strongly suggests that the gastric lesions are a manifestation of the same underlying disease process. The granulomas are composed of epithelioid histiocytes, multinucleated giant cells (Langhans type), and lymphocytes, lacking central necrosis (non-caseating).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): ***Helicobacter pylori* infection leading to chronic active gastritis with granuloma formation.** While *H. pylori* is a common cause of gastritis and can sometimes be associated with granuloma formation (especially in response to treatment or in specific subtypes), the biopsy explicitly states *H. pylori* is absent. Furthermore, *H. pylori* gastritis typically involves chronic inflammation, neutrophilic infiltrates, and active inflammation, not primarily non-caseating epithelioid granulomas without ulceration. This option is incorrect because *H. pylori* was ruled out and the histology doesn't fit typical *H. pylori* gastritis.
- (B): **Crohn disease manifesting with skip lesions involving the stomach.** Crohn disease is another cause of non-caseating granulomas, and it can affect any part of the GI tract, including the stomach (though less common than ileum/colon). However, gastric Crohn's disease often presents with ulceration, strictures, or fistulas, and typically involves transmural inflammation. While isolated gastric Crohn's can occur, the patient has confirmed systemic sarcoidosis, making sarcoidosis a much more likely explanation for the gastric granulomas. The absence of ulceration also makes Crohn's less typical. This option is incorrect because the patient has confirmed sarcoidosis, which is a more parsimonious explanation.
- (C): **Tuberculosis causing gastric involvement.** Gastric tuberculosis is rare but can occur, usually secondary to pulmonary TB. It typically presents with ulceration, often in the antrum or pylorus, and the granulomas are usually caseating (containing central necrosis), although non-caseating forms can occur. The negative AFB stain makes TB less likely, although sensitivity is not 100%. However, the confirmed diagnosis of sarcoidosis makes TB an unlikely cause of these specific granulomas. This option is incorrect because the granulomas are described as non-caseating, AFB stains are negative, and the patient has confirmed sarcoidosis.
- (E): **Idiopathic granulomatous gastritis unrelated to systemic disease.** Idiopathic granulomatous gastritis is a diagnosis of exclusion, considered when no underlying cause (infection, systemic disease) can be identified. Given the patient's confirmed diagnosis of systemic sarcoidosis, attributing the gastric granulomas to an unrelated idiopathic process is incorrect. The gastric lesions are almost certainly a manifestation of the known systemic disease. This option is incorrect because a specific systemic cause (sarcoidosis) has been identified.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** Non-caseating epithelioid granulomas in the GI tract can be caused by sarcoidosis, Crohn disease, infections (TB, fungi), or be idiopathic. When present, a thorough systemic evaluation is crucial to identify an underlying cause, especially sarcoidosis or Crohn's. The presence of confirmed systemic sarcoidosis strongly implicates it as the cause of GI granulomas.
- **Differentials:** Key differentials for gastric non-caseating granulomas include sarcoidosis, Crohn disease, tuberculosis, fungal infections, and idiopathic granulomatous gastritis. Distinguishing features include caseation (TB), ulceration/transmural inflammation (Crohn's), systemic findings (sarcoidosis, Crohn's, TB), and specific stains (AFB, GMS).

<a id='question-134'></a>

### Question 134: Idiopathic Granulomatous Mastitis
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Idiopathic Granulomatous Mastitis (IGM) is a benign, sterile inflammatory condition of the breast, primarily affecting parous women, that can clinically and radiologically mimic breast cancer, particularly inflammatory breast carcinoma. The key histological feature is non-caseating granulomatous inflammation centered on lobules (lobulitis). (特发性肉芽肿性乳腺炎是一种良性、无菌的乳腺炎症性疾病，主要影响已生育女性，在临床和放射学上可能模仿乳腺癌，特别是炎症性乳腺癌。关键组织学特征是围绕小叶（小叶炎）的无酪状肉芽肿性炎症。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old female, G2P2, presents to the breast clinic with a 4-cm palpable mass in the upper outer quadrant of her right breast. She reports that the mass has been growing over the past 6 weeks and is associated with tenderness, overlying skin erythema, and nipple retraction. She breastfed her last child 18 months ago. Physical examination reveals a firm, ill-defined mass with peau d'orange changes and nipple inversion. Mammography shows diffuse skin thickening and microlobulation, suspicious for malignancy. Ultrasound reveals a heterogeneous solid mass with irregular margins and posterior acoustic shadowing. A core needle biopsy was performed. Histopathology demonstrates dense non-caseating granulomatous inflammation centered on breast lobules, with associated microabscesses, epithelioid histiocytes, multinucleated giant cells, and scattered neutrophils. Special stains for acid-fast bacilli (AFB) and fungal elements are negative. Bacterial cultures of the biopsy tissue are also sterile. Which of the following is the most likely diagnosis?

(A) Invasive Ductal Carcinoma with prominent inflammatory infiltrate
(B) Granulomatous Mastitis secondary to bacterial infection (e.g., Staphylococcus aureus)
(C) Sarcoidosis involving the breast
(D) Idiopathic Granulomatous Mastitis
(E) Tuberculosis of the breast

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Pathophysiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 32-year-old parous female (G2P2) who recently breastfed (18 months ago). This demographic is characteristic of Idiopathic Granulomatous Mastitis (IGM).
- **Clinical Presentation:** Firm, tender, growing mass (4 cm) with skin erythema, peau d'orange, and nipple retraction. These features strongly mimic inflammatory breast carcinoma (IBC), a highly aggressive form of breast cancer.
- **Imaging Findings:** Mammography shows diffuse skin thickening and microlobulation; Ultrasound shows a heterogeneous solid mass with irregular margins and posterior acoustic shadowing. These findings are highly suspicious for malignancy, further supporting the mimicry of IBC.
- **Biopsy Findings:** Core needle biopsy reveals dense non-caseating granulomatous inflammation centered on breast lobules (lobulitis), microabscesses, epithelioid histiocytes, multinucleated giant cells, and neutrophils. This specific pattern of granulomatous inflammation targeting the lobules is the hallmark of IGM.
- **Negative Cultures:** Bacterial, fungal, and AFB stains/cultures are sterile, ruling out infectious causes like bacterial mastitis, fungal mastitis, or tuberculosis.
- **Synthesis:** The combination of a clinical presentation mimicking IBC in a parous woman, suspicious imaging, and characteristic histological findings (non-caseating granulomatous lobulitis) with negative infectious workup points strongly towards Idiopathic Granulomatous Mastitis.

##### 2. Pathophysiological Deep-Dive (病理生理机制深度剖析)
Idiopathic Granulomatous Mastitis (IGM) is a rare, benign, self-limiting inflammatory condition of the breast. The exact etiology is unknown (hence "idiopathic"), but it is strongly associated with recent pregnancy and lactation, particularly in parous women. It is hypothesized to be an autoimmune or hypersensitivity reaction, possibly triggered by retained milk, duct ectasia, or an aberrant immune response to breast tissue changes post-lactation. The key pathological feature is the presence of non-caseating granulomas centered on the breast lobules (lobulitis), often accompanied by microabscesses, epithelioid histiocytes, multinucleated giant cells, and neutrophils. This granulomatous inflammation can cause significant tissue destruction and fibrosis, leading to mass formation, skin changes (erythema, peau d'orange), and nipple retraction, clinically mimicking inflammatory breast carcinoma or a breast tumor. The inflammation is sterile, meaning standard bacterial, fungal, and mycobacterial cultures are negative. Treatment typically involves corticosteroids or, in severe cases, surgery, and the condition usually resolves spontaneously over time. The pathogenesis involves an inflammatory response, likely autoimmune in nature, targeting the lobular structures of the breast, leading to granuloma formation and tissue damage.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Invasive Ductal Carcinoma with prominent inflammatory infiltrate: While invasive ductal carcinoma (IDC) is the most common type of breast cancer, and some variants can have a desmoplastic stromal reaction or associated inflammation, the hallmark histological finding here is granulomatous inflammation centered on lobules, not malignant epithelial cells invading the stroma. The clinical presentation can mimic IBC, but the biopsy findings are definitive for a non-malignant process. The granulomatous inflammation is not typical for IDC, although rarely, a tumor can elicit a granulomatous response. However, the primary pathology described is granulomatous lobulitis.
- (B) Granulomatous Mastitis secondary to bacterial infection (e.g., Staphylococcus aureus): Bacterial mastitis, often caused by Staphylococcus aureus, typically presents as an acute, painful, erythematous breast condition, often associated with lactation (lactational mastitis). Histologically, it shows acute inflammation with neutrophils and abscess formation, potentially with bacterial organisms visible on Gram stain or culture. While granulomas can sometimes form in response to chronic bacterial infection, the primary finding in this case is dense non-caseating granulomatous lobulitis, and bacterial cultures are sterile, making a primary bacterial etiology unlikely.
- (C) Sarcoidosis involving the breast: Sarcoidosis is a systemic granulomatous disease of unknown etiology. Breast involvement is rare but can occur. Histologically, sarcoid granulomas are typically non-caseating, well-formed, and often contain multinucleated giant cells. However, sarcoid granulomas usually involve the stroma diffusely or are centered on ducts or adipose tissue, not specifically the lobules (lobulitis). Furthermore, sarcoidosis is a systemic disease, and the patient presentation is highly localized to the breast, mimicking a localized inflammatory process or malignancy rather than systemic sarcoidosis.
- (E) Tuberculosis of the breast: Tuberculous mastitis is a rare condition, usually resulting from hematogenous spread or direct extension from adjacent lymph nodes. Histologically, it typically shows caseating granulomas with acid-fast bacilli (AFB) present. The caseating nature of the granulomas and the presence of AFB are key distinguishing features from IGM, where the granulomas are non-caseating and AFB stains are negative, as confirmed in the vignette.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Idiopathic Granulomatous Mastitis (IGM) is a benign inflammatory condition mimicking breast cancer, especially in parous women. Key features include clinical/radiological suspicion for malignancy and histology showing non-caseating granulomatous lobulitis with sterile cultures. Differentiate IGM from infectious mastitis (positive cultures, acute presentation), tuberculosis (caseating granulomas, positive AFB), sarcoidosis (systemic disease, different granuloma location), and breast cancer (malignant cells, lack of characteristic granulomatous lobulitis).

<a id='question-135'></a>

### Question 135: Granulomatous Orchitis: Idiopathic vs Tuberculous Testicular Swelling
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Differentiating idiopathic granulomatous orchitis from tuberculous orchitis based on histopathological features, specifically the presence or absence of caseous necrosis and the underlying etiology (autoimmune vs. infectious). (区分特发性肉芽肿性睾丸炎与结核性睾丸炎的病理特征，特别是干酪样坏死的存在与否以及潜在的病因（自身免疫性 vs 感染性）。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 60-year-old male presents to his physician with a 3-month history of a painless, firm mass in his right testicle. He denies any history of trauma, fever, night sweats, weight loss, or urinary symptoms. His past medical history is unremarkable, and he takes no medications. Physical examination reveals a solitary, non-tender, hard mass approximately 4 cm in diameter occupying the upper pole of the right testicle. The left testicle is normal. Serum tumor markers (AFP, β-hCG, LDH) are within normal limits. Ultrasound imaging shows a heterogeneous solid mass within the right testicle, suspicious for malignancy. Due to the clinical suspicion for testicular cancer, a radical inguinal orchiectomy is performed. Gross examination of the specimen reveals a firm, tan-white mass replacing the upper pole of the testis. Microscopic examination shows extensive destruction of the seminiferous tubules, replaced by sheets of epithelioid histiocytes, lymphocytes, and numerous multinucleated giant cells surrounding degenerate spermatozoa. There is no evidence of caseous necrosis or neoplastic germ cells. Special stains for acid-fast bacilli (AFB) and fungi are negative. What is the most likely diagnosis?

(A) Testicular Seminoma
(B) Tuberculous Orchitis
(C) Idiopathic Granulomatous Orchitis
(D) Testicular Lymphoma
(E) Leydig Cell Tumor

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Pathophysiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 60-year-old male. Age is relevant as testicular cancer (seminoma) is more common in younger men (15-35), while idiopathic granulomatous orchitis is more common in middle-aged to older men.
- **Presentation:** Painless, firm, unilateral testicular mass developing over 3 months. This presentation is highly suspicious for testicular malignancy, particularly seminoma.
- **Negative Symptoms:** Absence of systemic symptoms (fever, night sweats, weight loss) makes infectious causes like tuberculosis less likely, although not impossible.
- **Normal Tumor Markers:** AFP, β-hCG, and LDH are normal, which argues against non-seminomatous germ cell tumors (NSGCTs) and some types of seminoma (though pure seminoma can have normal markers).
- **Imaging:** Ultrasound shows a suspicious solid mass.
- **Surgery:** Radical inguinal orchiectomy performed due to suspicion of malignancy.
- **Gross Pathology:** Firm, tan-white mass replacing testicular tissue. Non-specific finding.
- **Microscopic Pathology:** Key findings:
    - Destruction of seminiferous tubules.
    - Replacement by epithelioid histiocytes, lymphocytes, and multinucleated giant cells. This indicates a granulomatous inflammatory process.
    - Granulomas surrounding degenerate spermatozoa.
    - **Absence of caseous necrosis.** This is crucial for differentiating from tuberculosis.
    - **Absence of neoplastic germ cells.** This rules out seminoma and NSGCTs.
- **Special Stains:** Negative AFB and fungal stains rule out infectious granulomatous causes like tuberculosis and fungal infections.
- **Diagnosis Synthesis:** The combination of a testicular mass in an older male, granulomatous inflammation without caseous necrosis, negative infectious stains, and absence of malignancy points strongly towards idiopathic granulomatous orchitis.

##### 2. Pathophysiological Deep-Dive (病理生理机制深度剖析)
- **Idiopathic Granulomatous Orchitis (IGO):** This is a benign condition characterized by granulomatous inflammation within the testis. The pathogenesis is thought to be an autoimmune reaction triggered by microtrauma leading to the extravasation of sperm antigens. The immune system then mounts a granulomatous response against these self-antigens. Histologically, it presents as non-caseating granulomas composed of epithelioid histiocytes, lymphocytes, and multinucleated giant cells, often centered around degenerating sperm or tubules. It typically affects middle-aged to older men and presents as a painless testicular mass, mimicking malignancy clinically and radiologically. The absence of caseous necrosis and negative stains for infectious agents are key distinguishing features.
- **Tuberculous Orchitis:** This is an infectious granulomatous inflammation caused by *Mycobacterium tuberculosis*. It typically presents with testicular pain, swelling, and often systemic symptoms like fever and weight loss. Histologically, it shows granulomas with characteristic **caseous necrosis** (a cheesy, amorphous necrotic center) and positive AFB stains. The clinical presentation and histological features (especially caseous necrosis) are distinct from IGO.
- **Testicular Seminoma:** A malignant germ cell tumor. Histologically characterized by sheets of uniform, large cells with clear cytoplasm, prominent nucleoli, and fibrous septa infiltrated by lymphocytes. Tumor markers (β-hCG, AFP) may be elevated. The vignette explicitly states the absence of neoplastic germ cells.
- **Testicular Lymphoma:** Usually a secondary involvement from systemic lymphoma. Histologically shows infiltration by mature lymphocytes, often diffuse.
- **Leydig Cell Tumor:** A stromal tumor arising from Leydig cells. Histologically shows polygonal cells with abundant eosinophilic cytoplasm, round nuclei, and often crystalloid inclusions.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Testicular Seminoma: This is the most common testicular malignancy and often presents as a painless mass in young men. While the clinical presentation (painless mass) is similar, the histopathology described (granulomatous inflammation, absence of neoplastic germ cells) definitively rules out seminoma. Seminoma would show sheets of large, uniform cells with clear cytoplasm and prominent nucleoli. The normal tumor markers also make seminoma less likely, although not impossible.
- (B) Tuberculous Orchitis: This is an infectious granulomatous condition. While it presents with granulomas, the key distinguishing feature is the presence of **caseous necrosis** within the granulomas, which was explicitly absent in the description. Furthermore, the patient lacked systemic symptoms typical of tuberculosis, and AFB stains were negative.
- (D) Testicular Lymphoma: This is typically a secondary involvement from systemic lymphoma. Histology would show infiltration by lymphocytes, not the epithelioid histiocytes and giant cells characteristic of granulomas seen in this case. The clinical presentation can be similar (painless mass), but the pathology is distinct.
- (E) Leydig Cell Tumor: This is a stromal tumor. Histologically, it would show polygonal cells with abundant eosinophilic cytoplasm, often with crystalloid inclusions, not granulomatous inflammation. It typically presents as a mass but the histological features are completely different.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Idiopathic granulomatous orchitis is a benign condition mimicking testicular cancer in middle-aged/older men. The key histological feature is non-caseating granulomatous inflammation without evidence of malignancy or infection (negative AFB/fungal stains). Differentiating it from tuberculous orchitis (which shows caseous necrosis and positive AFB) and testicular seminoma (which shows neoplastic germ cells) is crucial. Always consider IGO in the differential diagnosis of a testicular mass in an older male, especially when tumor markers are normal and histology shows granulomas without caseation.

<a id='question-136'></a>

### Question 136: Giant Cell (Temporal) Arteritis
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Giant cell arteritis (GCA) is a systemic vasculitis affecting medium and large arteries, particularly the extracranial branches of the carotid artery (like the temporal artery), characterized by granulomatous inflammation targeting the internal elastic lamina, leading to fragmentation and luminal narrowing. It typically occurs in individuals over 50 years old and is often associated with polymyalgia rheumatica. (大细胞动脉炎是一种影响中大型动脉的全身性血管炎，特别是颈动脉的颅外分支（如颞动脉），其特征是针对内弹力板的肉芽肿性炎症，导致内弹力板碎片化和管腔狭窄。它通常发生在 50 岁以上的个体中，并且经常与多肌痛风湿症相关。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 72-year-old female presents to the emergency department complaining of a severe, throbbing headache localized to her right temporal region for the past two weeks. She also reports significant tenderness of her scalp, particularly when brushing her hair, and pain in her jaw that worsens with chewing (jaw claudication). She denies visual changes but reports low-grade fevers and generalized fatigue. Her past medical history is unremarkable except for hypertension controlled with lisinopril. Vital signs show a temperature of 37.8°C (100.0°F), blood pressure 140/85 mmHg, heart rate 88 bpm, and respiratory rate 16 breaths/min. Physical examination reveals a firm, nodular, and exquisitely tender right temporal artery, which is pulseless upon palpation. Laboratory investigations reveal an elevated erythrocyte sedimentation rate (ESR) of 110 mm/hr (normal <20 mm/hr) and an elevated C-reactive protein (CRP) of 65 mg/L (normal <10 mg/L). A temporal artery biopsy is performed. Which of the following histological findings is most characteristic of the underlying pathology?

(A) Fibrinoid necrosis of vessel walls with neutrophilic infiltrate and leukocytoclasia.
(B) Granulomatous inflammation with multinucleated giant cells and fragmentation of the internal elastic lamina, centered on the vessel lumen.
(C) Intimal thickening with lipid-laden macrophages (xanthoma cells) and smooth muscle cell proliferation.
(D) Vasculitis characterized by immune complex deposition in the vessel walls, leading to segmental fibrinoid necrosis and neutrophilic infiltration.
(E) Necrotizing inflammation of the vessel wall with transmural infiltration by lymphocytes, eosinophils, and plasma cells, often associated with cytoplasmic ANCA.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Pathophysiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Demographics:** 72-year-old female. This age (>50) is a major risk factor for Giant Cell Arteritis (GCA).
- **Symptoms:** Severe, unilateral temporal headache, scalp tenderness, jaw claudication (pain with chewing), low-grade fevers, fatigue. These are classic symptoms of GCA, particularly involving the temporal artery and branches of the external carotid artery. Jaw claudication is highly specific.
- **Physical Exam:** Firm, nodular, tender, pulseless right temporal artery. This finding strongly suggests inflammation and occlusion of the temporal artery.
- **Laboratory Findings:** Markedly elevated ESR (110 mm/hr) and CRP (65 mg/L). These are non-specific markers of inflammation but are typically very high in active GCA.
- **Diagnosis:** The constellation of symptoms, physical findings, and laboratory results in an elderly patient points strongly towards Giant Cell Arteritis (Temporal Arteritis).
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the *most characteristic* histological finding in a temporal artery biopsy.

##### 2. Pathophysiological Deep-Dive (病理生理机制深度剖析)
- GCA is a systemic vasculitis primarily affecting medium and large arteries, especially branches of the carotid artery (temporal, ophthalmic) and aorta.
- The hallmark pathological feature is transmural (affecting all layers of the vessel wall) granulomatous inflammation.
- This inflammation is centered on the internal elastic lamina (IEL).
- Key cellular components include lymphocytes, macrophages, and multinucleated giant cells (hence the name "Giant Cell Arteritis").
- The inflammatory process leads to destruction and fragmentation of the IEL, which is crucial for maintaining vessel wall integrity and elasticity. Duplication of the IEL can also occur as the body attempts to repair the damage.
- This damage to the IEL and vessel wall leads to luminal stenosis, thrombosis, and potentially occlusion, explaining the pulseless artery and symptoms like jaw claudication and visual loss (if ophthalmic arteries are involved).
- Therefore, the characteristic histological finding is granulomatous inflammation with giant cells and fragmentation/duplication of the internal elastic lamina.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Fibrinoid necrosis of vessel walls with neutrophilic infiltrate and leukocytoclasia. This describes the typical histology of small vessel vasculitides, such as polyarteritis nodosa (PAN) or microscopic polyangiitis (MPA), often associated with immune complex deposition or ANCA. GCA primarily affects large/medium vessels and features granulomatous inflammation, not fibrinoid necrosis and leukocytoclasia.
- (B) Granulomatous inflammation with multinucleated giant cells and fragmentation of the internal elastic lamina, centered on the vessel lumen. This accurately describes the classic histological features of Giant Cell Arteritis (Temporal Arteritis). The inflammation targets the IEL, leading to its fragmentation, and involves giant cells within a granulomatous background.
- (C) Intimal thickening with lipid-laden macrophages (xanthoma cells) and smooth muscle cell proliferation. This describes the histological features of atherosclerosis. While GCA can occur in arteries affected by atherosclerosis, the primary pathology of GCA is vasculitis, not atherosclerosis.
- (D) Vasculitis characterized by immune complex deposition in the vessel walls, leading to segmental fibrinoid necrosis and neutrophilic infiltration. This describes immune complex-mediated vasculitis, such as cryoglobulinemic vasculitis or Henoch-Schönlein purpura (HSP). GCA is not primarily mediated by immune complexes, and the inflammation is granulomatous, not characterized by fibrinoid necrosis and neutrophilic infiltration.
- (E) Necrotizing inflammation of the vessel wall with transmural infiltration by lymphocytes, eosinophils, and plasma cells, often associated with cytoplasmic ANCA. This describes the histology of granulomatosis with polyangiitis (GPA, formerly Wegener's granulomatosis), a type of ANCA-associated vasculitis. While GPA involves granulomatous inflammation, it typically affects small-to-medium vessels (especially in the respiratory tract and kidneys), involves eosinophils, and is associated with c-ANCA. GCA affects large/medium vessels, lacks significant eosinophils, and is not typically ANCA-associated.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Giant Cell Arteritis (Temporal Arteritis) is characterized by granulomatous inflammation targeting the internal elastic lamina of medium and large arteries in patients >50 years old. The key histological finding is fragmentation of the internal elastic lamina along with giant cells. It is strongly associated with Polymyalgia Rheumatica. Differential diagnoses include other vasculitides (PAN, GPA, MPA), atherosclerosis, and infections.

---

<a id='question-137'></a>

### Question 137: Giant Cell Arteritis Complications: Anterior Ischemic Optic Neuropathy (AION)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Giant cell arteritis (GCA) can cause inflammation and occlusion of the ophthalmic artery and its branches (short posterior ciliary arteries), leading to anterior ischemic optic neuropathy (AION), characterized by sudden, painless vision loss and optic disc pallor/edema. (大细胞动脉炎可导致眼动脉及其分支（短后房睫状动脉）炎症和闭塞，引起前缺血性视神经病变，其特征是突发、无痛的视力丧失和视盘苍白/水肿。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 74-year-old woman presents to the emergency department complaining of sudden, painless, complete vision loss in her left eye, which she describes as "a black curtain falling." She has a history of polymyalgia rheumatica, treated with low-dose aspirin. On examination, her visual acuity in the left eye is counting fingers at 1 foot. Fundoscopic examination of the left eye reveals a pale, swollen optic disc with indistinct margins. Her erythrocyte sedimentation rate (ESR) is markedly elevated at 110 mm/hr (normal <20 mm/hr), and her C-reactive protein (CRP) is also significantly elevated. Temporal artery palpation reveals tenderness and decreased pulsation on the left side. Which of the following pathophysiological mechanisms most directly explains this patient's acute vision loss?

(A) Vasculitis-induced inflammation and compression of the optic chiasm, leading to bitemporal hemianopia.
(B) Occlusion of the central retinal artery by an embolus originating from an inflamed temporal artery, causing retinal whitening and a cherry-red spot.
(C) Granulomatous inflammation and occlusion of the short posterior ciliary arteries supplying the optic nerve head, resulting in ischemic infarction of the anterior portion of the optic nerve.
(D) Direct inflammatory infiltration of the optic nerve sheath, causing demyelination and conduction block, similar to optic neuritis.
(E) Vasculitis-induced retinal vein occlusion, leading to retinal hemorrhages, cotton wool spots, and macular edema.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Pathophysiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 74-year-old woman - Age > 50 is a key risk factor for Giant Cell Arteritis (GCA).
- **Presenting Complaint:** Sudden, painless, complete vision loss ("black curtain") in one eye - Classic presentation of Anterior Ischemic Optic Neuropathy (AION). Painless nature distinguishes it from optic neuritis. Sudden onset suggests vascular occlusion.
- **Past Medical History:** Polymyalgia rheumatica (PMR) - Strongly associated with GCA (occurs in ~50% of GCA patients).
- **Physical Exam:**
    - Visual acuity: Counting fingers - Severe vision loss.
    - Fundoscopy: Pale, swollen optic disc with indistinct margins - Characteristic findings of AION due to ischemia and edema of the optic nerve head.
- **Labs:** Elevated ESR (110 mm/hr) and CRP - Hallmark systemic inflammatory markers elevated in GCA.
- **Temporal Artery Exam:** Tenderness and decreased pulsation - Suggestive of temporal arteritis.
- **Diagnosis:** The constellation of symptoms (age, sudden painless vision loss, PMR history, elevated inflammatory markers, temporal artery tenderness) strongly points towards GCA complicated by AION.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the *pathophysiological mechanism* of the *acute vision loss*.

##### 2. Pathophysiological Deep-Dive (病理生理机制深度剖析)
- GCA is a systemic vasculitis affecting medium and large arteries, particularly branches of the carotid artery, including the temporal artery and the ophthalmic artery.
- The ophthalmic artery gives rise to the short posterior ciliary arteries (SPCAs), which are the primary blood supply to the optic nerve head (the anterior portion of the optic nerve).
- In GCA, granulomatous inflammation infiltrates the walls of these arteries (temporal, ophthalmic, SPCAs).
- This inflammation leads to vessel wall thickening, stenosis, and ultimately occlusion of the affected arteries.
- Occlusion of the SPCAs deprives the optic nerve head of its blood supply, causing ischemic infarction.
- This ischemic damage to the anterior optic nerve fibers results in AION, characterized by sudden, painless vision loss and the characteristic fundoscopic findings (pale, swollen disc).
- Therefore, the direct mechanism of vision loss in this scenario is granulomatous inflammation and occlusion of the short posterior ciliary arteries supplying the optic nerve head, leading to ischemic infarction of the anterior optic nerve.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Vasculitis-induced inflammation and compression of the optic chiasm, leading to bitemporal hemianopia.
    - **Incorrect:** GCA typically affects the arteries supplying the optic nerve head (AION) or the retina (Central Retinal Artery Occlusion - CRAO), not typically the optic chiasm directly via compression. Chiasmal lesions usually cause visual field defects (like bitemporal hemianopia) rather than sudden, complete monocular vision loss. AION affects the optic nerve head, not the chiasm.
- (B): Occlusion of the central retinal artery by an embolus originating from an inflamed temporal artery, causing retinal whitening and a cherry-red spot.
    - **Incorrect:** This describes Central Retinal Artery Occlusion (CRAO). While GCA can cause CRAO (due to emboli from the temporal or ophthalmic artery), the fundoscopic findings described (pale, swollen disc) are characteristic of AION, not CRAO (which shows retinal whitening and a cherry-red spot). AION affects the optic nerve head, while CRAO affects the inner retina.
- (D): Direct inflammatory infiltration of the optic nerve sheath, causing demyelination and conduction block, similar to optic neuritis.
    - **Incorrect:** Optic neuritis involves inflammation *within* the optic nerve itself (demyelination), often associated with multiple sclerosis. It typically presents with pain on eye movement and subacute vision loss. GCA causes *ischemia* due to vascular occlusion, not direct inflammatory demyelination of the nerve sheath. The fundoscopic findings also differ (often normal or with mild disc hyperemia in optic neuritis, vs. pale/swollen disc in AION).
- (E): Vasculitis-induced retinal vein occlusion, leading to retinal hemorrhages, cotton wool spots, and macular edema.
    - **Incorrect:** Retinal vein occlusion (RVO) affects the veins draining the retina. While GCA can potentially affect retinal veins, the primary and most common cause of acute vision loss in GCA is AION due to arterial occlusion. RVO presents with different fundoscopic findings (hemorrhages, cotton wool spots, edema) and often a more gradual onset of vision loss compared to the sudden loss seen in AION.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Giant Cell Arteritis (GCA) is a vasculitis affecting medium/large arteries, especially cranial branches of the aorta. A major complication is AION, caused by occlusion of the short posterior ciliary arteries supplying the optic nerve head, leading to sudden, painless vision loss and a pale, swollen optic disc. Prompt treatment with high-dose steroids is crucial to prevent contralateral vision loss. Key differentials for sudden vision loss include CRAO, retinal detachment, and optic neuritis, each with distinct clinical and fundoscopic features.

---

<a id='question-138'></a>

### Question 138: Takayasu Arteritis
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Large-vessel vasculitis affecting the aorta and its major branches, characterized by granulomatous inflammation, typically seen in young Asian women. (大血管炎，影响主动脉及其主要分支，特征是肉芽肿性炎症，通常见于年轻亚洲女性)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 26-year-old Asian female presents to the clinic complaining of increasing exertional fatigue and lightheadedness over the past 6 months. She also reports intermittent claudication in both arms, particularly when lifting objects. Her past medical history is unremarkable, and she denies smoking or illicit drug use. On physical examination, her blood pressure is 140/80 mmHg in her right arm and 105/70 mmHg in her left arm. Radial pulses are weak and difficult to palpate bilaterally. Auscultation reveals prominent systolic bruits over both subclavian arteries and the carotid arteries. Laboratory tests show an elevated erythrocyte sedimentation rate (ESR) of 85 mm/hr (normal <20 mm/hr) and C-reactive protein (CRP) of 60 mg/L (normal <10 mg/L). Magnetic resonance angiography (MRA) of the chest and neck reveals marked concentric smooth narrowing and wall thickening involving the aortic arch, brachiocephalic trunk, and the origins of the left subclavian and left common carotid arteries. A biopsy of a resected segment of the affected aorta shows transmural inflammation with prominent lymphocytes, macrophages, and multinucleated giant cells within the media and adventitia, along with significant fibrosis. Which of the following pathological processes is most characteristic of this patient's condition?

(A) Fibrinoid necrosis and neutrophil infiltration of small vessel walls, leading to palpable purpura and glomerulonephritis.
(B) Granulomatous inflammation involving the intima, media, and adventitia of medium-sized arteries, often associated with polyangiitis (formerly Wegener's granulomatosis).
(C) Mononuclear cell infiltration and fibrosis of the adventitia and media of large arteries, particularly the aorta and its major branches, often leading to stenosis or aneurysm formation.
(D) Immune complex deposition in small vessel walls, causing leukocytoclastic vasculitis, typically presenting with skin lesions and arthritis.
(E) Necrotizing inflammation of medium-sized arteries, predominantly affecting the renal arteries and mesenteric vessels, often associated with anti-neutrophil cytoplasmic antibodies (ANCA).

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Pathophysiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Demographics:** 26-year-old Asian female. This demographic is highly characteristic of Takayasu arteritis, which predominantly affects young women (<40 years old), particularly those of Asian descent.
- **Symptoms:** Exertional fatigue, lightheadedness, arm claudication. These symptoms suggest reduced blood flow to the upper extremities due to stenosis of the major arteries supplying them.
- **Physical Exam:** Weak radial pulses bilaterally ("pulseless disease"), significant blood pressure discrepancy between arms (>20 mmHg difference), prominent bruits over subclavian/carotid arteries. These findings strongly indicate stenosis or occlusion of the aortic arch branches (subclavian, carotid) and the aorta itself, leading to differential flow and turbulent blood flow.
- **Labs:** Elevated ESR and CRP. These are non-specific markers of inflammation, consistent with a systemic inflammatory process like vasculitis.
- **Imaging (MRA):** Concentric smooth narrowing and wall thickening of the aortic arch and origins of brachiocephalic and subclavian vessels. This confirms large-vessel involvement with stenosis, a hallmark of Takayasu arteritis.
- **Histopathology:** Transmural inflammation (media and adventitia) with lymphocytes, macrophages, and giant cells, along with fibrosis. This describes the characteristic granulomatous panarteritis seen in Takayasu arteritis, affecting all layers of the large vessel wall.

##### 2. Pathophysiological Deep-Dive (病理生理机制深度剖析)
Takayasu arteritis is a chronic granulomatous vasculitis affecting large arteries, primarily the aorta and its main branches (e.g., subclavian, carotid, renal arteries). The pathogenesis involves an autoimmune response triggered by unknown factors (possibly infectious or genetic predisposition) in genetically susceptible individuals. The inflammatory process targets the adventitia, media, and intima of the affected vessels.
- **Initiating Stimulus:** Unknown, possibly infectious trigger or genetic susceptibility in young women.
- **Immune Response:** Activation of T-cells (particularly Th1 and Th17) and macrophages.
- **Inflammation:** Infiltration of lymphocytes, macrophages, and multinucleated giant cells into the vessel wall (panarteritis), particularly the media and adventitia. This leads to the formation of granulomas.
- **Vascular Damage:** Chronic inflammation causes destruction of the elastic fibers and smooth muscle cells in the media.
- **Fibrosis:** Healing involves extensive fibrosis, leading to thickening and stiffening of the vessel wall.
- **Clinical Consequences:** The inflammation and subsequent fibrosis cause stenosis (narrowing), occlusion (blockage), or aneurysm formation in the affected arteries. In this case, stenosis of the aortic arch branches leads to reduced blood flow to the upper extremities, causing arm claudication, weak pulses, and blood pressure discrepancies. The systemic inflammation results in elevated ESR and CRP. The histopathology confirms the characteristic granulomatous inflammation involving all layers of the large vessel wall.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Describes Polyarteritis Nodosa (PAN) or microscopic polyangiitis (MPA). PAN affects medium-sized arteries, while MPA affects small vessels. Both typically involve fibrinoid necrosis and neutrophil infiltration, not primarily granulomas, and are not typically associated with the specific demographic or large vessel distribution seen here. Palpable purpura and glomerulonephritis are more characteristic of small vessel vasculitis (like MPA or IgA vasculitis).
- (B): Describes Granulomatosis with Polyangiitis (GPA, formerly Wegener's granulomatosis). GPA is a systemic vasculitis characterized by granulomatous inflammation of the respiratory tract (upper and lower) and necrotizing granulomatous vasculitis of small-to-medium sized vessels. While it involves granulomas, it typically affects smaller vessels and involves the respiratory tract prominently, which is not the primary feature here. The patient's presentation is classic for large vessel involvement.
- (D): Describes Leukocytoclastic Vasculitis (LCV), a type of small vessel vasculitis often caused by immune complex deposition. It typically presents with palpable purpura, arthritis, and sometimes systemic symptoms. Histologically, it shows fibrinoid necrosis and neutrophil infiltration in small vessel walls, not the granulomatous panarteritis of large vessels seen in this case.
- (E): Describes Polyarteritis Nodosa (PAN) or Microscopic Polyangiitis (MPA) associated with ANCA. PAN affects medium-sized arteries, often involving renal and mesenteric vessels, and typically shows necrotizing arteritis without granulomas. MPA affects small vessels and is also ANCA-associated. Neither fits the large vessel, granulomatous, aortic arch involvement seen in Takayasu arteritis.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Takayasu arteritis is a large-vessel granulomatous vasculitis primarily affecting young Asian women, leading to stenosis or occlusion of the aorta and its major branches. Key features include "pulseless disease," blood pressure discrepancies between limbs, and elevated inflammatory markers. Histopathology shows granulomatous inflammation involving all layers of the vessel wall. Differential diagnoses include other large vessel vasculitides like Giant Cell Arteritis (typically older patients, temporal artery involvement) and atherosclerosis (less inflammatory, different imaging/histology).

<a id='question-139'></a>

### Question 139: Granulomatosis with Polyangiitis (GPA / Wegener)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Granulomatosis with Polyangiitis (GPA) is an ANCA-associated vasculitis characterized by necrotizing granulomatous inflammation of the upper and lower respiratory tracts and necrotizing glomerulonephritis. (GPA 是一种 ANCA 相关性血管炎，其特征是上呼吸道和下呼吸道的坏死性肉芽肿性炎症以及坏死性肾小球肾炎。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 48-year-old male presents to the emergency department with a 6-month history of recurrent purulent sinusitis, worsening nasal congestion, and recent onset of epistaxis. He reports a chronic cough productive of blood-tinged sputum (hemoptysis) for the past 3 months. Physical examination reveals a depressed nasal bridge ("saddle-nose deformity") and bibasilar crackles on lung auscultation. Laboratory tests show elevated serum creatinine (3.5 mg/dL) and urinalysis reveals hematuria with red blood cell casts and significant proteinuria. A chest CT scan demonstrates multiple bilateral pulmonary nodules, several of which exhibit central cavitation. Renal biopsy reveals pauci-immune crescentic glomerulonephritis. Which of the following pathological processes is most characteristic of this patient's condition?

(A) Immune complex deposition in glomerular mesangium and capillary loops, leading to membranoproliferative glomerulonephritis.
(B) Fibrinoid necrosis of small vessel walls with neutrophil infiltration and granuloma formation in the respiratory tract, accompanied by pauci-immune crescentic glomerulonephritis.
(C) Deposition of anti-glomerular basement membrane (anti-GBM) antibodies, causing linear IgG deposition along the GBM and rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis.
(D) Vasculitis affecting medium-sized arteries, leading to aneurysm formation, thrombosis, and infarction, typically seen in the temporal artery or aorta.
(E) Deposition of amyloid fibrils in the glomeruli, interstitium, and vessel walls, resulting in nephrotic syndrome and characteristic Congo red staining.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Pathophysiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **48-year-old male:** Age is typical for ANCA-associated vasculitides.
- **Recurrent purulent sinusitis, nasal congestion, epistaxis, saddle-nose deformity:** Classic upper respiratory tract involvement in GPA, indicating necrotizing granulomatous inflammation. Saddle-nose deformity is a specific late finding.
- **Chronic cough, hemoptysis:** Lower respiratory tract involvement, suggesting vasculitis and potential cavitation.
- **Elevated creatinine, hematuria, RBC casts, proteinuria:** Acute renal failure with nephritic syndrome, pointing towards glomerulonephritis.
- **Chest CT: multiple bilateral cavitary lung nodules:** Highly characteristic finding in GPA, resulting from necrotizing vasculitis and granulomatous inflammation in the lung parenchyma.
- **Renal biopsy: pauci-immune crescentic glomerulonephritis:** The hallmark renal manifestation of ANCA-associated vasculitis, including GPA. "Pauci-immune" means minimal or no immune complex deposition.
- **Convergence:** The combination of upper respiratory tract granulomatous inflammation (sinusitis, saddle nose), lower respiratory tract necrotizing vasculitis with cavitation (hemoptysis, cavitary nodules), and pauci-immune crescentic glomerulonephritis strongly points to Granulomatosis with Polyangiitis (GPA, formerly Wegener's granulomatosis). The question asks for the characteristic pathological process.

##### 2. Pathophysiological Deep-Dive (病理生理机制深度剖析)
GPA is a systemic necrotizing vasculitis primarily affecting small-to-medium sized vessels. It is strongly associated with anti-neutrophil cytoplasmic antibodies (ANCA), particularly c-ANCA (anti-PR3). The pathogenesis involves:
1.  **ANCA binding:** ANCA binds to neutrophils, activating them.
2.  **Neutrophil activation:** Activated neutrophils adhere to endothelial cells, release reactive oxygen species (ROS) and proteolytic enzymes (e.g., elastase, proteinase-3).
3.  **Endothelial damage:** This leads to endothelial injury, vasculitis, and vessel wall necrosis (fibrinoid necrosis).
4.  **Granuloma formation:** In the respiratory tract, this process is often accompanied by granuloma formation (collections of activated macrophages, lymphocytes, and multinucleated giant cells) around the necrotic vessels.
5.  **Organ damage:** This vasculitis causes tissue ischemia and necrosis in affected organs.
    *   **Upper respiratory tract:** Necrotizing granulomatous inflammation leads to sinusitis, nasal ulceration, septal perforation, and cartilage destruction (saddle-nose deformity).
    *   **Lower respiratory tract:** Necrotizing granulomatous vasculitis affects pulmonary capillaries and small vessels, leading to alveolar hemorrhage, interstitial inflammation, and cavitation of nodules.
    *   **Kidneys:** Vasculitis affects glomerular capillaries, leading to fibrinoid necrosis, crescent formation (proliferation of parietal epithelial cells and infiltration of inflammatory cells into Bowman's space), and pauci-immune glomerulonephritis (minimal immune complex deposition).
Therefore, the characteristic pathological process involves fibrinoid necrosis of small vessel walls with neutrophil infiltration and granuloma formation in the respiratory tract, accompanied by pauci-immune crescentic glomerulonephritis.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Immune complex deposition in glomerular mesangium and capillary loops, leading to membranoproliferative glomerulonephritis.** This describes immune complex-mediated glomerulonephritis, such as post-infectious glomerulonephritis, lupus nephritis, or IgA nephropathy. These conditions typically show prominent immune complex deposition (IgG, IgM, C3) on immunofluorescence and electron microscopy, which is absent ("pauci-immune") in GPA. Membranoproliferative glomerulonephritis (MPGN) is a specific pattern characterized by mesangial hypercellularity, endocapillary proliferation, and subendothelial/mesangial deposits, not typical for GPA.
- (C): **Deposition of anti-glomerular basement membrane (anti-GBM) antibodies, causing linear IgG deposition along the GBM and rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis.** This describes Goodpasture syndrome (or anti-GBM disease). It is characterized by linear IgG deposition along the glomerular basement membrane on immunofluorescence, unlike the pauci-immune pattern of GPA. While both cause rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis, the underlying mechanism and immunofluorescence pattern are distinct.
- (D): **Vasculitis affecting medium-sized arteries, leading to aneurysm formation, thrombosis, and infarction, typically seen in the temporal artery or aorta.** This describes large vessel vasculitis, such as Giant Cell Arteritis (temporal arteritis) or Takayasu arteritis. These conditions affect larger arteries and do not typically cause the upper/lower respiratory tract granulomas or pauci-immune glomerulonephritis seen in GPA.
- (E): **Deposition of amyloid fibrils in the glomeruli, interstitium, and vessel walls, resulting in nephrotic syndrome and characteristic Congo red staining.** This describes amyloidosis, a condition involving the deposition of misfolded proteins (amyloid fibrils). Renal amyloidosis typically presents with nephrotic syndrome (heavy proteinuria, edema, hypoalbuminemia) rather than nephritic syndrome (hematuria, RBC casts, hypertension) and does not involve vasculitis or granulomas. The characteristic diagnostic feature is Congo red staining with apple-green birefringence under polarized light.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
GPA is characterized by the triad of upper respiratory tract involvement (granulomatous inflammation), lower respiratory tract involvement (necrotizing vasculitis, cavitation), and glomerulonephritis (pauci-immune crescentic). Key differentials include other ANCA-associated vasculitides (Microscopic Polyangiitis - MPA, Eosinophilic Granulomatosis with Polyangiitis - EGPA), Goodpasture syndrome (anti-GBM disease), and other forms of glomerulonephritis. Remember the characteristic pauci-immune pattern on renal biopsy and the presence of granulomas in the respiratory tract for GPA.

<a id='question-140'></a>

### Question 140: Granulomatosis with Polyangiitis (Wegener's)
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Association of specific serological markers (PR3-ANCA/c-ANCA) with specific systemic vasculitides, particularly Granulomatosis with Polyangiitis (GPA). (特定血清学标志物（PR3-ANCA/c-ANCA）与特定全身性血管炎的相关性，特别是肉芽肿性多血管炎（GPA）。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 58-year-old male presents to the emergency department with a 3-month history of progressive shortness of breath, cough productive of blood-tinged sputum, and worsening nasal congestion with epistaxis. He reports recent onset of bilateral lower extremity purpura and joint pain. Past medical history is unremarkable except for hypertension controlled with lisinopril. Physical examination reveals saddle nose deformity, bilateral conjunctivitis, and scattered palpable purpura on the lower extremities. Laboratory studies show elevated erythrocyte sedimentation rate (ESR) and C-reactive protein (CRP). Urinalysis reveals hematuria and proteinuria. A biopsy of the patient's lung tissue shows necrotizing granulomatous inflammation with vasculitis affecting small to medium-sized vessels. Indirect immunofluorescence (IIF) testing on ethanol-fixed neutrophils shows a diffuse cytoplasmic staining pattern. Subsequent ELISA testing confirms the presence of antibodies targeting a 29-kDa serine protease found within neutrophil primary granules. Which of the following autoantibodies is most likely responsible for the patient's clinical presentation?

(A) Anti-glomerular basement membrane (anti-GBM) antibodies
(B) Anti-neutrophil cytoplasmic antibodies (ANCA) targeting myeloperoxidase (MPO-ANCA / p-ANCA)
(C) Anti-neutrophil cytoplasmic antibodies (ANCA) targeting proteinase 3 (PR3-ANCA / c-ANCA)
(D) Anti-endothelial cell antibodies (AECA)
(E) Rheumatoid factor (RF)

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Pathophysiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 58-year-old male. Age is typical for GPA onset.
- **Symptoms:** Progressive shortness of breath, cough with hemoptysis, nasal congestion, epistaxis, lower extremity purpura, joint pain. This constellation points towards a systemic vasculitis affecting the respiratory tract (upper and lower), kidneys (hematuria, proteinuria), and skin (purpura).
- **Physical Exam:** Saddle nose deformity (chronic upper airway involvement), conjunctivitis, palpable purpura. These findings are highly suggestive of Granulomatosis with Polyangiitis (GPA).
- **Labs:** Elevated ESR/CRP (inflammation), hematuria/proteinuria (glomerulonephritis).
- **Biopsy:** Necrotizing granulomatous inflammation with vasculitis affecting small-to-medium vessels in the lung. This is the hallmark histological finding of GPA.
- **Serology:** IIF on neutrophils shows diffuse cytoplasmic staining (c-ANCA pattern). ELISA confirms antibodies against a 29-kDa serine protease in neutrophil primary granules. This specifically identifies Proteinase 3 (PR3).
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the autoantibody responsible for the clinical presentation, given the specific serological findings.

##### 2. Pathophysiological Deep-Dive (病理生理机制深度剖析)
- **Granulomatosis with Polyangiitis (GPA):** A systemic necrotizing vasculitis primarily affecting small-to-medium vessels, characterized by granulomatous inflammation, often involving the upper respiratory tract, lower respiratory tract, and kidneys.
- **ANCA:** Anti-neutrophil cytoplasmic antibodies are autoantibodies directed against antigens within neutrophils. They are strongly associated with certain types of systemic vasculitis.
- **ANCA Patterns:**
    - **c-ANCA (cytoplasmic ANCA):** Diffuse granular cytoplasmic staining on IIF. The primary target antigen is Proteinase 3 (PR3), a 29-kDa serine protease stored in the azurophilic (primary) granules of neutrophils. c-ANCA/PR3-ANCA is highly specific (~95%) for GPA.
    - **p-ANCA (perinuclear ANCA):** Staining appears around the nucleus on IIF. The most common target antigen is Myeloperoxidase (MPO), a 80-kDa enzyme found in neutrophil granules. p-ANCA/MPO-ANCA is associated with microscopic polyangiitis (MPA), eosinophilic granulomatosis with polyangiitis (EGPA/Churg-Strauss), and sometimes GPA (less common, ~10%). Other antigens can cause p-ANCA (e.g., elastase, cathepsin G, lactoferrin).
- **Mechanism in GPA:** PR3-ANCA antibodies bind to Proteinase 3 on the surface of neutrophils and endothelial cells. This binding activates neutrophils, leading to the release of reactive oxygen species (ROS), proteases, and other inflammatory mediators. These mediators damage endothelial cells, causing vasculitis and vessel wall destruction. The granulomatous inflammation is also thought to be driven by activated neutrophils and macrophages. The specific clinical manifestations (sinusitis, pulmonary infiltrates, glomerulonephritis) result from the vasculitis and inflammation affecting these organs. The vignette describes classic GPA features (upper/lower respiratory tract, kidney, skin involvement, saddle nose) along with the characteristic c-ANCA/PR3-ANCA serology.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Anti-glomerular basement membrane (anti-GBM) antibodies:** These antibodies target the alpha-3 chain of type IV collagen in the glomerular basement membrane. They cause Goodpasture syndrome (rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis and pulmonary hemorrhage). While the patient has glomerulonephritis, the upper respiratory tract involvement (sinusitis, saddle nose), granulomatous inflammation on biopsy, and c-ANCA pattern are not typical for anti-GBM disease. Anti-GBM disease typically shows linear IgG deposition on immunofluorescence microscopy of the kidney biopsy.
- (B) **Anti-neutrophil cytoplasmic antibodies (ANCA) targeting myeloperoxidase (MPO-ANCA / p-ANCA):** MPO-ANCA is associated with microscopic polyangiitis (MPA) and eosinophilic granulomatosis with polyangiitis (EGPA). MPA typically presents with rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis and pulmonary capillaritis (diffuse alveolar hemorrhage) but lacks the granulomatous inflammation and prominent upper respiratory tract involvement seen in GPA. EGPA is characterized by asthma, eosinophilia, and systemic vasculitis. The patient's clinical picture (granulomas, saddle nose, upper airway disease) and the specific c-ANCA pattern strongly point away from MPO-ANCA.
- (D) **Anti-endothelial cell antibodies (AECA):** These antibodies target antigens on endothelial cells and are found in various vasculitides, including GPA, MPA, and cryoglobulinemic vasculitis. However, they are not as specific as ANCA for diagnosing GPA, and the vignette provides specific information about the ANCA pattern and target antigen (PR3), making PR3-ANCA the most direct and specific answer. AECA are less commonly tested and less diagnostically specific than ANCA in this context.
- (E) **Rheumatoid factor (RF):** RF is an autoantibody (typically IgM) directed against the Fc portion of IgG. It is primarily associated with rheumatoid arthritis but can be found in other autoimmune diseases and chronic infections. It is not specifically associated with GPA or ANCA-associated vasculitis. The patient's presentation is not typical for rheumatoid arthritis.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** c-ANCA (cytoplasmic pattern on IIF) is highly specific for Granulomatosis with Polyangiitis (GPA), and the target antigen is Proteinase 3 (PR3). p-ANCA (perinuclear pattern) is most commonly associated with MPO and Microscopic Polyangiitis (MPA).
- **Differentials:** Key differentials for ANCA-associated vasculitis include Microscopic Polyangiitis (MPA, typically p-ANCA/MPO-ANCA, lacks granulomas), Eosinophilic Gran

<a id='question-141'></a>

### Question 141: Eosinophilic Granulomatosis with Polyangiitis (EGPA / Churg-Strauss)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The classic triad of severe asthma, allergic rhinitis/sinusitis (often with nasal polyps), and peripheral eosinophilia, along with characteristic vasculitis affecting small-to-medium vessels, particularly in the context of mononeuritis multiplex, is diagnostic for Eosinophilic Granulomatosis with Polyangiitis (EGPA / Churg-Strauss syndrome). (严重哮喘、过敏性鼻炎/鼻窦炎（常伴鼻息肉）和外周嗜酸性粒细胞增多症的三联征，以及伴有单神经炎复发性血管炎的小到中等血管受累，是嗜酸性粒细胞肉芽肿性多血管炎（EGPA / Churg-Strauss综合征）的诊断。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 38-year-old male with a lifelong history of severe, poorly controlled allergic asthma and chronic allergic rhinosinusitis complicated by nasal polyps presents to the emergency department. For the past week, he has experienced worsening shortness of breath requiring increased use of his rescue inhaler. Over the preceding 3 days, he developed progressive weakness in his right hand, culminating in an inability to extend his wrist (wrist drop). Subsequently, he noticed similar weakness developing in his left foot, resulting in foot drop. Physical examination reveals diffuse wheezing, nasal polyps, and marked tenderness along the course of the peroneal nerve on the left side. Laboratory studies show a total white blood cell count of 11,900/uL with 32% eosinophils (absolute eosinophil count 3,800/uL). A sural nerve biopsy demonstrates necrotizing vasculitis involving small vessels, accompanied by dense perivascular eosinophilic infiltrates and extravascular necrotizing granulomas. Which of the following pathophysiological mechanisms is most directly responsible for the patient's peripheral neuropathy?

(A) Immune complex deposition leading to complement activation and neutrophil recruitment.
(B) Direct cytotoxic effects of eosinophil-derived major basic protein (MBP) and eosinophil cationic protein (ECP) on Schwann cells and nerve axons.
(C) Formation of anti-neuronal antibodies targeting voltage-gated potassium channels, leading to demyelination.
(D) Fibrinoid necrosis of vessel walls caused by deposition of cryoglobulins, resulting in nerve ischemia.
(E) Activation of microglia and astrocytes leading to chronic inflammation and demyelination within the central nervous system.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Pathophysiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 38-year-old male. Age is typical for EGPA onset (often 4th-5th decade).
- **History:** Lifelong severe allergic asthma, chronic allergic rhinosinusitis with nasal polyps. This is the classic "prodromal phase" of EGPA, characterized by atopy.
- **Presenting Symptoms:** Worsening asthma, progressive weakness (wrist drop, foot drop) over 3 days. Wrist drop and foot drop are classic signs of mononeuritis multiplex, indicating asymmetric peripheral nerve involvement.
- **Physical Exam:** Diffuse wheezing (asthma), nasal polyps (rhinosinusitis), peroneal nerve tenderness (neuropathy).
- **Labs:** Marked peripheral eosinophilia (3,800/uL; 32% of WBC). This is a hallmark feature of EGPA.
- **Biopsy:** Sural nerve biopsy showing necrotizing small-vessel vasculitis, perivascular eosinophilic infiltration, and extravascular necrotizing granulomas. This confirms the diagnosis of EGPA, specifically showing the characteristic vasculitic and eosinophilic/granulomatous inflammation affecting peripheral nerves.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism responsible for the *peripheral neuropathy*.

The combination of severe asthma, rhinosinusitis with polyps, peripheral eosinophilia, and mononeuritis multiplex with biopsy findings of eosinophilic vasculitis strongly points to EGPA. The question specifically asks about the mechanism causing the neuropathy in this context.

##### 2. Pathophysiological Deep-Dive (病理生理机制深度剖析)
- **EGPA Pathogenesis:** EGPA is a systemic necrotizing vasculitis affecting small-to-medium sized vessels, characterized by asthma, eosinophilia, and granulomas. It is often associated with ANCA, particularly MPO-ANCA (anti-myeloperoxidase).
- **Neuropathy in EGPA:** Peripheral neuropathy, often presenting as mononeuritis multiplex (asymmetric involvement of multiple individual nerves), is a common manifestation. This occurs because the vasculitis directly affects the vasa nervorum (blood vessels supplying the nerves).
- **Mechanism of Nerve Damage:** The vasculitis leads to inflammation and damage to the vessel walls, causing ischemia and infarction of the nerve tissue. Crucially, eosinophils play a central role. Eosinophils release toxic granule proteins, including major basic protein (MBP) and eosinophil cationic protein (ECP), which are directly cytotoxic to Schwann cells (responsible for myelination in the peripheral nervous system) and axons. This direct toxicity, combined with ischemic damage from vasculitis, leads to demyelination and axonal loss, resulting in the clinical signs of neuropathy (weakness, sensory loss). The biopsy findings (vasculitis, eosinophils, granulomas) confirm this process is occurring in the patient's peripheral nerves.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Immune complex deposition leading to complement activation and neutrophil recruitment. This mechanism is characteristic of diseases like systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE) vasculitis or post-streptococcal glomerulonephritis. While immune complexes can be involved in some vasculitides, the *primary* driver of nerve damage in EGPA is eosinophil-mediated toxicity and vasculitis, not predominantly immune complex deposition leading to neutrophil recruitment in the nerves. Neutrophils are not the characteristic inflammatory cell in EGPA vasculitis; eosinophils are.
- (C): Formation of anti-neuronal antibodies targeting voltage-gated potassium channels, leading to demyelination. This mechanism is characteristic of Lambert-Eaton myasthenic syndrome (LEMS) or certain forms of Guillain-Barré syndrome (GBS), which are autoimmune neuropathies. While autoimmunity is involved in EGPA (ANCA), the neuropathy is primarily driven by vasculitis and direct eosinophil toxicity, not anti-neuronal antibodies targeting potassium channels.
- (D): Fibrinoid necrosis of vessel walls caused by deposition of cryoglobulins, resulting in nerve ischemia. Cryoglobulinemic vasculitis is associated with conditions like hepatitis C infection or certain lymphoproliferative disorders. While vasculitis causes ischemia in EGPA, the underlying cause is not cryoglobulin deposition. Fibrinoid necrosis can occur, but the hallmark is eosinophilic infiltration and granulomas, not cryoglobulins.
- (E): Activation of microglia and astrocytes leading to chronic inflammation and demyelination within the central nervous system. This describes the pathophysiology of diseases affecting the central nervous system (CNS), such as multiple sclerosis (MS). EGPA primarily affects the peripheral nervous system (PNS) through vasculitis of the vasa nervorum and direct eosinophil toxicity, not the CNS via microglial/astrocyte activation.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** Remember the triad of asthma, eosinophilia, and vasculitis (often affecting nerves, lungs, skin, heart) for EGPA. The neuropathy is caused by eosinophil-mediated vasculitis of the vasa nervorum and direct eosinophil toxicity to nerves.
- **Differentials:** Other causes of mononeuritis multiplex include vasculitides (like polyarteritis nodosa, though less commonly associated with asthma/eosinophilia), diabetes mellitus, infections (e.g., Lyme disease, leprosy), and infiltrative diseases (e.g., amyloidosis). However, the specific combination of asthma, eosinophilia, and biopsy findings points strongly to EGPA.
